Therefore, these two cases can be simplified as:

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}_{t}^{LB}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\max\left(P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})\cdot P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a}),P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})\cdot(1-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{s_{t+1}\}}{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})})\right)}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

\displaystyle=\max\left(P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a}),1-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{s_{t+1}\}}{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}\right)

∎

Case 2:
\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\neq s_{t+1}
,
\dfrac{P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}\geq\dfrac{P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}
and
P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})>0

Proof.

Because
\dfrac{P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}\geq\dfrac{P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}
and
P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})>0
, to satisfy CS (
15
)
\widetilde{P}_{t}^{LB}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})=0
. We have already proven in Case 2 of the proof for Theorem
D.5
that there exists an assignment of
\theta
that satisfies all the constraints, where
\widetilde{P}_{t}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})=0
. Therefore, the same assignment of
\theta
will satisfy the constraints of the optimisation problem, and result in
\widetilde{P}_{t}^{LB}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})=0
.
∎

Case 3:
\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\neq s_{t+1}
, and
\dfrac{P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}<\dfrac{P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}
or
P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})=0

Proof.

Consider the following disjoint cases:

•

1-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}\geq 0

•

1-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}<0

Case 3(a):
1-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}\geq 0

Proof.

We can assign
\theta
as follows:

\begin{cases}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}=P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})\cdot(1-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})})\\
\forall{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}},\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}=P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})\cdot{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}\\
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}=P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}\\
\forall{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}},\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}=P(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}\\
\end{cases}

This assignment of
\theta
satisfies the constraints of the linear optimisation problem, as follows:

•

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}\\
=P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})
, satisfying (
12
).

•

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}\\
=1-P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})
, satisfying (
12
).

•

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}=P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
,

satisfying (
12
).

•

\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}\\
=1-P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}P(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
, therefore it is possible to assign
\theta
such that
\forall s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\},\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}=P(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
, satisfying (
12
).

•

\forall s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\},\widetilde{P}_{t}(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})=\dfrac{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}=\dfrac{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})\cdot\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}=\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
, which we have already shown in the proof for Theorem
D.5
satisfies Mon1 (
13
), Mon2 (
14
) and CS (
15
).

•

Mon2 (
14
) is also satisfied for the transition
\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a}\rightarrow\widetilde{s}^{\prime}
, as follows:

–

If
P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})=0
, then this assignment of
\theta
vacuously satisfies Mon2 (
14
).

–

Otherwise, we have already proven that there exists an assignment of
\theta
that satisfies all the constraints (as proven in Theorem
D.5
). If
1-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}>0
, then the minimum possible counterfactual transition probability of
\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a}\rightarrow\widetilde{s}^{\prime}
is
\widetilde{P}_{t}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})=1-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}}{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}
(because the upper bounds must sum to
1
). Therefore,
1-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}\leq P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
, or there would be no valid assignment of
\theta
that satisfies Mon2 (
14
).

•

CS (
15
) will be vacuously satisfied for the transition
\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a}\rightarrow\widetilde{s}^{\prime}
, because
\dfrac{P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}<\dfrac{P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}
or
P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})=0
.

•

0\leq\theta_{u_{t}}\leq 1,\forall u_{t}
, satisfying (
16
).

•

\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\theta_{u_{t}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{s_{t+1}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}\\
+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{s_{t+1}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}=1
, satisfying (
17
).

All the constraints are satisfied, so this is a valid assignment of
\theta
for this state-action pair.
∎

Case 3(b):
1-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}<0

Proof.

Because
1-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}<0
, we have:

P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})\cdot\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}>P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})

Therefore, we can assign
\theta
as follows:

\begin{cases}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}=0\\
\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}=P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})\\
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}=P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})\\
\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}{P(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}\\
\end{cases}

This assignment of
\theta
satisfies the constraints of the linear optimisation problem, as follows:

•

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}\\
=P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})
, satisfying (
12
).

•

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}\\
=1-P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})
, satisfying (
12
).

•

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}=P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
,

satisfying (
12
).

•

\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}\\
=1-P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}P(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
, therefore it is possible to assign
\theta
such that
\forall s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\},\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}=P(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
, satisfying (
12
).

•

From this assignment of
\theta
,
\widetilde{P}_{t}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})=\dfrac{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}=\dfrac{0}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}=0
, which immediately satisfies the Mon2 (
14
) and CS (
15
) constraints for the transition
\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a}\rightarrow\widetilde{s}^{\prime}
.

•

Because
1-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}<0
:

P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})<\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})\cdot P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

This guarantees that we can assign
\theta
such that the Mon1 (
13
), Mon2 (
14
) and CS (
15
) constraints are satisfied for all transitions
\widetilde{s}^{\prime},\widetilde{a}^{\prime}\rightarrow s^{\prime},\forall s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}
. In particular, we should assign
\theta
such that
\widetilde{P}_{t}(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})=\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
(Theorem
D.5
proves this
\theta
exists), as we have proven that Mon1 (
13
) and CS (
15
) constraints are satisfied for
\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
. Then, we can assign
\theta
such that
\forall s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime},s_{t+1}\},\widetilde{P}_{t}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})\leq\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
, and because each
\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
has been proven to satisfy the Mon2 (
14
) and CS (
15
) constraints, each
\widetilde{P}_{t}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
must as well.

•

0\leq\theta_{u_{t}}\leq 1,\forall u_{t}
, satisfying (
16
).

•

\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\theta_{u_{t}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{s_{t+1}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\end{subarray}}\theta_{u_{t}}\\
+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{s_{t+1}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})\neq s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}=1
, satisfying (
17
).

All the constraints are satisfied, so this is a valid assignment of
\theta
for this state-action pair.

∎

Therefore, these two cases can be simplified as:

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}_{t}^{LB}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\max\left(0,P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})\cdot(1-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})})\right)}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

\displaystyle=\max\left(0,1-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\}}{\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}\right)

∎

We have proven all the cases for Theorem
D.6
, therefore Theorem
D.6
holds.

∎

D.2.
Any CFMDP Entailed by an Interval CFMDP is a Valid CFMDP

In our experiments, we evaluate the policies by sampling example CFMDPs from the interval CFMDPs. But, even if
\theta
s exist that would produce each sampled CF transition probability separately, this does not necessarily guarantee that there exists a single
\theta
that produces all of the CF transition probabilities in the CFMDP. We can prove that, given any CFMDP
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{t}
, there exists a
\theta
that produces all the counterfactual probabilities in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{t}
, as follows.

Theorem D.7.

Given any arbitrary interval counterfactual MDP, any counterfactual MDP
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{t}
entailed by this interval counterfactual MDP will be a valid counterfactual MDP.

Proof.

Let us assume that we have an interval CFMDP, and an arbitrary CFMDP
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}_{t}}
entailed by this interval CFMDP for each time-step
t
from this interval CFMDP. First, we need to prove that, for every transition
s,a\rightarrow s^{\prime}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}_{t}}
, there exists a
\theta
that produces its counterfactual probability
\widetilde{P}_{t}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)
. Next, we need to show that, for all state-action pairs
(s,a)
, there exists a
\theta
that satisfies the constraints of the optimisation problem (
12
)-(
17
) and produces the entailed CF probability distribution
\widetilde{P}_{t}(\cdot\mid s,a)
. Finally, similarly to the proof in Section
D.1
, we can prove by induction that these assignments of
\theta
for each state-action pair can be combined to form a single
\theta
that produces
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}_{t}}
.

D.2.1.
Existence of
\theta
for Each
\widetilde{P}_{t}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)

The counterfactual transition probability bounds are produced by a convex optimisation problem (or using the analytical bounds, which we have proven produce the same exact bounds as the optimisation problem). Because the optimisation problem is convex, the feasible set for each of the counterfactual transition probabilities is also convex (i.e., there are no gaps in the probability bounds). This means there is guaranteed to be at least one
\theta
that can produce each of the entailed counterfactual transition probabilities in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}_{t}}
.

D.2.2.
Existence of
\theta
for Each
\widetilde{P}_{t}(\cdot\mid s,a)

Next, we need to show that, for all state-action pairs
(s,a)
, there exists a
\theta
that satisfies the constraints of the optimisation problem (
12
)-(
17
) and can produce the entailed CF probability distribution
\widetilde{P}_{t}(\cdot\mid s,a)
. Take any state-action pair
(s,a)
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}_{t}}
arbitrarily. Each CF transition probability
\widetilde{P}_{t}(\cdot\mid s,a)
arises from the probability over the mechanisms that produce each transition
s,a\rightarrow s^{\prime}
and observed transition
s_{t},a_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1}
vs. the total probability assigned to
P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})
. Therefore, we need to show that the total probability assigned across all mechanisms containing each transition and the observed transition sums to
P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})
. Because
\widetilde{P}_{t}(\cdot\mid s,a)
is a valid probability distribution, we know

\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\widetilde{P}_{t}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)=1

This means

\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\dfrac{\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{s,a,u_{t},s^{\prime}}\cdot\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}=1

and

\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{s,a,u_{t},s^{\prime}}\cdot\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}={P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

Because
\mu_{s,a,u_{t},s^{\prime}}=1
for exactly one next state,
s^{\prime}
, and
0
for the remaining states, we must have

\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}={P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

so the total probability assigned across all mechanisms containing each transition and the observed transition sums to
P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})
.

Next, we need to show that there exists an assignment of
\theta
which satisfies the interventional probability constraints (
12
) of the optimisation problem. We know that the interventional probabilities of all transitions from
(s,a)
must sum to
1
:

\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}P(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)=1

Because
0\leq\theta_{u_{t}}\leq 1,\forall u_{t}
(
16
) and
\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\theta_{u_{t}}=1
(
17
) it must be possible to assign
\theta
such that

\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{s,a,u_{t},s^{\prime}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}=P(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)

and

\forall s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S},\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{s,a,u_{t},s^{\prime}}\cdot\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}=\widetilde{P}_{t}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)\cdot P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})

and

\forall s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S},\sum_{\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\neq s_{t+1}}\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{s,a,u_{t},s^{\prime}}\cdot\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},\widetilde{s}^{\prime}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}=P(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)-\widetilde{P}_{t}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)\cdot P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})

Therefore, this assignment satisfies

\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{s,a,u_{t},s^{\prime}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}=P(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)

\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}={P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

and

\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{s_{t+1}\}}\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s^{\prime}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}=1-P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})

which satisfies the interventional probability constraints (
12
). Finally, because each counterfactual transition probability was sampled from its bounds, we know that the entailed counterfactual transition probabilities satisfy the Mon1 (
13
), Mon (
14
) and CS (
15
) constraints as well.

D.2.3.
Existence of
\theta
for
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}_{t}}

We have shown that there exists a
\theta
that meets the constraints of the optimisation problem for each state-action pair separately in the MDP. Now,
we need to prove that these assignments can be combined to form a valid
\theta
for all state-action pairs. Let us prove inductively that adding a new state-action pair from the MDP to the causal model (and changing
\theta
to meet the constraints for this new state-action pair) does not affect whether the constraints of the other state-action pairs are satisfied. If this is the case, we can consider how
\theta
can be assigned to satisfy the constraints for each state-action pair separately, as we know these can be combined to form a valid assignment of
\theta
over all of the state-action pairs.

Base Case 1

Assume
(s_{t},a_{t})
(the observed state-action pair) is the only state-action pair currently considered in the causal model (this always exists, as we always have an observed transition). We require a structural equation mechanism for each possible next state (of which there are
|\mathcal{S}|
). This leads to an assignment of
\theta^{1}
as follows, which is the only possible assignment that satisfies the constraints (
12
)-(
17
):

\theta^{1}_{u_{1}}

\theta^{1}_{u_{2}}

…

\theta^{1}_{u_{|\mathcal{S}|}}

P(s_{1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})

P(s_{2}\mid s_{t},a_{t})

…

P(s_{|\mathcal{S}|}\mid s_{t},a_{t})

\theta^{1}=[P(s_{1}\mid s_{t},a_{t}),P(s_{2}\mid s_{t},a_{t}),...,P(s_{|\mathcal{S}|}\mid s_{t},a_{t})]
. Given
\theta^{1}
, the counterfactual transition probability for each transition
s_{t},a_{t}\rightarrow s^{\prime}
can be calculated with:

\widetilde{P}_{t}(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})=\dfrac{\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s^{\prime}}\cdot\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})},\forall s^{\prime}

Therefore, given
\theta^{1}
,
\widetilde{P}_{t}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})=1
, and
\forall s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{s_{t+1}\}
,
\widetilde{P}_{t}(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})=0
. This is the only possible counterfactual probability distribution for
(s_{t},a_{t})
, so this is guaranteed to be the distribution entailed from the interval CFMDP. These counterfactual probabilities also satisfy the monotonicity and counterfactual stability constraints (6-8) of the optimisation problem, as follows:

•

Because
\dfrac{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}=1
and
\forall s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S},\dfrac{P(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}{P(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}=1
,

\forall s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S},\dfrac{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}=\dfrac{P(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}{P(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

so CS (
15
) is always vacuously true for all transitions from
(s_{t},a_{t})
.

•

Because
\widetilde{P}_{t}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})=1\geq P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})
, this satisfies Mon1 (
13
).

•

Because
\forall s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{s_{t+1}\},\widetilde{P}_{t}(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})=0\leq P(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})
, this satisfies Mon2 (
14
).

Base Case 2

Let us assume that we only have the observed state-action pair in the causal model, and assume we have a valid
\theta^{1}
with
|\mathcal{S}|
structural equation mechanisms.

Now, take another state-action pair
(s,a)
arbitrarily from the MDP to add to the causal model. We now require
|\mathcal{S}|^{2}
structural equation mechanisms, one for each possible combination of transitions from the two state-action pairs. We can view this as separating each of the existing structural equation mechanisms from Base Case 1 into
|\mathcal{S}|
new structural equation mechanisms, one for every possible transition from the new state-action pair,
(s,a)
:

u_{1}
...
u_{|S|}
u_{1,1}
u_{1,|S|}
u_{1,2}
...
...
u_{|S|,1}
u_{|S|,|S|}
u_{|S|,2}

where, for
1\leq i\leq|\mathcal{S}|,1\leq n\leq|\mathcal{S}|
,
u_{i,n}
leads to the same next state for
(s_{t},a_{t})
as
u_{i}
, and produces the transition
s,a\rightarrow s_{n}
.

In the same way, we can split each
\theta^{1}_{u_{1}},...,\theta^{1}_{u_{|\mathcal{S}|}}
across these new structural equation mechanisms to obtain
\theta^{2}
:

\theta^{1}_{u_{1}}
...
\theta^{1}_{u_{|S|}}
\theta^{2}_{u_{1,1}}
\theta^{2}_{u_{1,|S|}}
\theta^{2}_{u_{1,2}}
...
...
\theta^{2}_{u_{|S|,1}}
\theta^{2}_{u_{|S|,|S|}}
\theta^{2}_{u_{|S|,2}}

By splitting
\theta^{1}
in this way, we guarantee that the total probability across each set of mechanisms (where each set produces a different transition from
(s_{t},a_{t})
) remains the same, i.e.,

\forall i\in\{1,..,|\mathcal{S}|\},\sum_{n=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|}\theta^{2}_{u_{i,n}}=\theta_{u_{i}}^{1}

This means that the counterfactual probabilities of all transitions from
(s_{t},a_{t})
will be exactly the same when using
\theta^{2}
as with
\theta^{1}
. Because we assume all the constraints were satisfied when using
\theta^{1}
, this guarantees that all the constraints of the optimisation problem will continue to hold for
(s_{t},a_{t})
with
\theta^{2}
.

Now, we only need to ensure we assign
\theta^{2}
such that all the constraints hold for the transitions from
(s,a)
. Firstly, we need
\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|}{\theta^{2}_{u_{i,n}}}=P(s_{n}\mid s,a)
for every possible next state
s_{n}\in\mathcal{S}
, to satisfy the interventional constraint (
12
). For each transition
s,a\rightarrow s_{n}
, there is a
\theta^{2}_{u_{i,n}}
for every
u_{i}
, and for every
\theta^{2}_{u_{i,n}}
,
0\leq\theta^{2}_{u_{i,n}}\leq\theta^{1}_{u_{n}}
. Therefore,
0\leq\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|}{\theta^{2}_{u_{i,n}}}\leq 1,\forall s_{n}\in\mathcal{S}
. We also know that:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|}\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|}\theta^{2}_{u_{i},n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|}\theta^{1}_{u_{i}}\text{ (because we split the probabilities of each $\theta^{1}_{u_{i}}$)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|}P(s_{i}|s_{t},a_{t})

\displaystyle=1

and

\sum_{n=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|}\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|}{\theta^{2}_{u_{i,n}}}=\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|}P(s_{i}|s,a)=1

Therefore, no matter the assignment of
\theta^{1}
, we can satisfy all the constraints (
12
)-(
17
) for all transitions from
(s,a)
. In Section
D.2.2
, we prove that, for any
(s,a)
, we can assign
\theta^{2}
such that it produces the entailed counterfactual probability distribution.

Inductive Case

Let us assume that we now have
k
state-action pair values from the MDP
\mathcal{M}
in our causal model, and we have found a valid assignment
\theta^{k}
that satisfies all the constraints for
k
state-action pairs from the MDP
\mathcal{M}
, including
(s_{t},a_{t})
. Because there are
k
state-action pairs, there are
|\mathcal{S}|^{k}
possible unique structural equation mechanisms for these state-action pairs
12
12

12

Note we have changed the indexing of each
\theta^{k}_{i,n}
to
\theta^{k}_{j}
, where
j=(i-1)\cdot|\mathcal{S}|+n
, with probabilities as follows:

\theta^{k}_{u_{1}}

…

\theta^{k}_{u_{|\mathcal{S}|}}

…

\theta^{k}_{u_{|\mathcal{S}|^{2}}}

…

\theta^{k}_{u_{|\mathcal{S}|^{k}}}

Now, we wish to add the
k+1^{th}
state-action pair from the MDP to the causal model, resulting in
|\mathcal{S}|^{k+1}
possible structural equation mechanisms. Let the
k+1^{th}
state-action pair be
(s,a)
arbitrarily. We can view this as separating the existing structural equation mechanisms each into
|\mathcal{S}|
new structural equation mechanisms, one for every possible transition from the
k+1^{th}
state-action pair:

u_{1}
...
u_{|S|}
...
u_{|S|^{2}}
u_{|S|^{k}}
...
u_{1,1}
u_{1,|S|}
u_{1,2}
...
u_{|S|,1}
u_{|S|,|S|}
u_{|S|,2}
...
u_{|S|^{2},1}
u_{|S|^{2},|S|}
u_{|S|^{2},2}
...
u_{|S|^{k},1}
u_{|S|^{k},|S|}
u_{|S|^{k},2}
...

where each
u_{i,n}
produces the same next states for all of the first
k
state-action pairs as
u_{i}
, and produces the transition
s,a\rightarrow s_{n}
.

In the same way, we can split each
\theta^{k}_{u_{1}},...,\theta^{k}_{u_{|\mathcal{S}|}},...
across these new structural equation mechanisms to find
\theta^{k+1}
.

\theta^{k}_{u_{1}}
...
\theta^{k}_{u_{|S|}}
...
\theta^{k}_{u_{|S|^{2}}}
\theta^{k}_{u_{|S|^{k}}}
...
\theta^{k+1}_{u_{1,1}}
\theta^{k+1}_{u_{1,|S|}}
\theta^{k+1}_{u_{1,2}}
...
\theta^{k+1}_{u_{|S|,1}}
\theta^{k+1}_{u_{|S|,|S|}}
\theta^{k+1}_{u_{|S|,2}}
...
\theta^{k+1}_{u_{|S|^{2},1}}
\theta^{k+1}_{u_{|S|^{2},|S|}}
\theta^{k+1}_{u_{|S|^{2},2}}
...
\theta^{k+1}_{u_{|S|^{k},1}}
\theta^{k+1}_{u_{|S|^{k},|S|}}
\theta^{k+1}_{u_{|S|^{k},2}}
...

By splitting
\theta^{k}
in this way, we have
\forall i\in\{1,..,|\mathcal{S}|\},\sum_{n=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|}\theta^{k+1}_{u_{i,n}}=\theta_{u_{i}}^{k}
. Each
\theta^{k+1}_{u_{i,n}}
is only different to
\theta^{k}_{u_{i}}
in the transition from
(s,a)
. Therefore, this guarantees that for every state-action pair
(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\neq(s,a)
in the first
k
pairs added to the causal model, the total probability across each set of mechanisms (where each set produces a different transition from
(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})
) remains the same in
\theta^{k+1}
as in
\theta^{k}
. As a result, the counterfactual probabilities of all transitions from each
(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})
will be exactly the same when using
\theta^{k+1}
as with
\theta^{k}
. Because we assume all the constraints were satisfied when using
\theta^{k}
, this guarantees that all the constraints of the optimisation problem will continue to hold for
(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})
with
\theta^{k+1}
.

Now, we only need to make sure that we can assign
\theta^{k+1}
such that the constraints also hold for all the transitions from
(s,a)
. We need
\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|^{k}}{\theta^{k+1}_{u_{i,n}}}=P(s_{n}\mid s,a)
for every possible next state
s_{n}\in\mathcal{S}
, to satisfy the interventional probability constraints (
12
). For each transition
s,a\rightarrow s_{n}
, there is a
\theta^{k+1}_{u_{i,n}}
for every
u_{i}
, and for every
\theta^{k+1}_{u_{i,n}}
,
0\leq\theta^{k+1}_{u_{i,n}}\leq\theta^{k}_{u_{n}}
, therefore
0\leq\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|^{k}}{\theta^{k+1}_{u_{i,n}}}\leq 1,\forall s_{n}\in\mathcal{S}
. We also know that:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|}\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|^{k}}\theta^{k+1}_{u_{i},n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|^{k}}\theta^{k}_{u_{i}}\text{ (because we split the probabilities of each $\theta^{k}_{u_{i}}$)}

\displaystyle=1

and

\sum_{n=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|}\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|^{k}}{\theta^{2}_{u_{i,n}}}=\sum_{n=1}^{|\mathcal{S}|}P(s_{n}|s,a)=1

Therefore, no matter the assignment of
\theta^{k}
, we can satisfy the interventional probability constraints (
12
) for all transitions from
(s,a)
. In Section
D.2.2
, we prove that, for any
(s,a)
, we can assign
\theta^{k+1}
such that it produces the entailed counterfactual probability distribution (which we prove always satisfies the constraints of the optimisation problem). Therefore, we know we can assign
\theta^{k+1}
such that it produces the counterfactual probability distributions for all
k+1
state-action pairs in the causal model.

Conclusion

By induction, we have proven that as long as we can find a
\theta
that produces the entailed counterfactual probability distribution for each state-action pair separately, we can combine these into a single valid
\theta
that produces the entailed CFMDP
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}_{t}}
.
∎

D.3.
Lemmas

Lemma D.0.

If
(s_{t},a_{t})\neq(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
and
(s_{t},a_{t})
and
(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
have disjoint support, the monotonicity constraints (
13
) and (
14
) and counterfactual stability (
15
) will be satisfied for all possible counterfactual transition probabilities for transitions from
(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
.

Proof.

Because
(s_{t},a_{t})
and
(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
have disjoint support,
P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})=0
and, for all
\widetilde{s}^{\prime}
where
P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})>0
,
P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})=0
.
For all
\widetilde{s}^{\prime}
where
P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})>0
,
P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})=0
, so CS (
15
) and Mon2 (
14
) will be vacuously true for all transitions from
(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
. Also, because
P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})=0
, Mon1 (
13
) is vacuously true.
∎

Lemma D.0.

For any observed transition
s_{t},a_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1}
and counterfactual transition
\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a}\rightarrow\widetilde{s}^{\prime}
:

\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t},\widetilde{s}^{\prime}}\cdot\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}\leq\min(P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t}),P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a}))

Proof.

From our interventional probability constraints (
12
), we have:

(37)

\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}=P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})

and

(38)

\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t},\widetilde{s}^{\prime}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}=P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})

\mu_{\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u,\widetilde{s}^{\prime}}\in\{0,1\}
(from its definition).
\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t},\widetilde{s}^{\prime}}\cdot\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}
is maximal when the probability assigned to all
u_{t}
where
\mu_{\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t},\widetilde{s}^{\prime}}=1
and
\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}=1
is maximal.

\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t},\widetilde{s}^{\prime}}\cdot(\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}})\leq\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}=P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})

\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}(\mu_{\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t},\widetilde{s}^{\prime}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}})\cdot\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\leq\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t},\widetilde{s}^{\prime}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}=P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})

Because of Eq. (
37
) and Eq. (
38
), the probability assigned to all
u_{t}
where
\mu_{\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t},\widetilde{s}^{\prime}}=1
and
\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}=1
cannot be greater than
P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})
or
P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
. Therefore,

\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t},\widetilde{s}^{\prime}}\cdot\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}\leq\min(P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t}),P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a}))

This also means that the upper bound of any counterfactual transition probability

\widetilde{P}_{t}^{UB}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})\leq\dfrac{\min(P(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a}),P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t}))}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}
.
∎

Lemma D.0.

For any observed transition
s_{t},a_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1}
,
\widetilde{P}_{t}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})=1
and,
\forall\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
where
\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{s_{t+1}\}
,
\widetilde{P}_{t}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})=0
.

Proof.

From (
18
), we have:

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}_{t}(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\cdot\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

(from the interventional probability constraint (
12
))

\displaystyle=\frac{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

(since
s_{t},a_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1}
was observed,
P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})>0
)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}_{t}(\widetilde{s}^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{u_{t}=1}^{|U_{t}|}\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},\widetilde{s}^{\prime}}\cdot\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}\cdot\theta_{u_{t}}}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

(since at most one of
\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},\widetilde{s}^{\prime}}
and
\mu_{s_{t},a_{t},u_{t},s_{t+1}}
can equal 1)

\displaystyle=\frac{0}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

(since
s_{t},a_{t}\rightarrow s_{t+1}
was observed,
P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})>0
)

\displaystyle=0

These results are as expected, because, given the same exogenous noise and input, we must get the same output in the counterfactual world.
∎

Lemma D.0.

Take an arbitrary counterfactual state-action pair
(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
and observed state-action pair from an MDP, where
(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})\neq(s_{t},a_{t})
and
(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
has overlapping support with
(s_{t},a_{t})
and
P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})<P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})
. Let
s_{t+1}
be the observed next state. It can be shown that:

\max_{\theta}\left(\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{s_{t+1}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}\geq P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})-P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})\right)

Proof.

First, let
S_{CS}\subset\mathcal{S}\setminus\{s_{t+1}\}
be the set of states
s^{\prime}\in S_{CS}
which must have a counterfactual transition probability
\widetilde{P}_{t}(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})=0
because of the counterfactual stability constraint (i.e., where
\forall s^{\prime}\in S_{CS},\dfrac{P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}\geq\dfrac{P(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}
and
P(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})>0
). Consider the total interventional probability of across all states
s^{\prime}\in S_{CS}
.
\forall s^{\prime}\in S_{CS}
:

\displaystyle\dfrac{P(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}\text{ (from CS \eqref{proofeq:counterfactual stability})}

\displaystyle P(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})\cdot P(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

The sum of the interventional probabilities of these transitions is:

\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S_{CS}}P(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})\leq\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S_{CS}}\dfrac{P(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})\cdot P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

Let
S_{other}
be the set of all other states
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus{\{s_{t+1}\}\cup S_{CS}}
. We can calculate the sum of the interventional probabilities of the transitions going to states
s^{\prime}\in S_{other}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S_{other}}P(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})

\displaystyle=1-P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})-\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in S_{CS}}P(s^{\prime\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})

\displaystyle\geq 1-P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})-\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in S_{CS}}\dfrac{P(s^{\prime\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})\cdot P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

\displaystyle=1-P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})-\dfrac{P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}\cdot\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in S_{CS}}P(s^{\prime\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})

\displaystyle=1-P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})

\displaystyle-\dfrac{P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}\cdot(1-P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})-\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S_{other}}P(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t}))

\displaystyle=1-P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})-\dfrac{P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

\displaystyle+P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})+\dfrac{P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})\cdot\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S_{other}}P(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

\displaystyle=1-\dfrac{P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}+\dfrac{P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})\cdot\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S_{other}}P(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}{P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})}

Because of the Mon2 (
14
) and CS (
15
) constraints, we know that
\forall s^{\prime\prime}\in S_{CS},\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}=0
, and
s^{\prime}\in S_{other}
, we have to assume
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}\leq P(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})\cdot P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})
as we cannot know which states are affected by the Mon2 constraint. So,

\max_{\theta}\left(\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in S_{CS}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}\right)=0

\max_{\theta}\left(\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S_{other}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}\right)\leq\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S_{other}}P(s^{\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})\cdot P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})

Therefore, the maximum
\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{s_{t+1}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}
is as follows:

(39)

\displaystyle\max_{\theta}\left(\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{s_{t+1}\}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\max_{\theta}\left(\sum_{s^{\prime\prime}\in S_{CS}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}\right)+\max_{\theta}\left(\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S_{other}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u_{t}\in U_{t}\\
f(s_{t},a_{t},u_{t})=s_{t+1}\\
f(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a},u_{t})=s^{\prime}\end{subarray}}{\theta_{u_{t}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S_{other}}P(s^{\prime\prime}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})\cdot P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})

\displaystyle\geq P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})-P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})+P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})\cdot\sum_{s^{\prime}\in S_{other}}P(s^{\prime}\mid s_{t},a_{t})

\displaystyle\geq P(s_{t+1}\mid s_{t},a_{t})-P(s_{t+1}\mid\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})

∎

Appendix E
Equivalence with Existing Work

Li and Pearl (2024)
’s bounds for the probability of causation are provided below. We can show that these are equivalent to our analytical bounds where the support of the counterfactual state-action pair
(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
is disjoint from the support of the observed state-action pair
(s_{t},a_{t})
, given in Theorems
D.3
and
D.4
.

Theorem E.1 (Bounds for Probability of Causation).

Suppose X has m values
x_{1},...,x_{m}
and Y has n values
y_{1},...,y_{n}
, the probability of causation
P(y_{i_{x_{j}}},y_{k},x_{p})
where
1\leq i
,
k\leq n
,
1\leq j
,
p\leq m
,
j\neq p
is given by:

P^{LB}(y_{i_{x_{j}}},y_{k},x_{p})=\max(0,P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})+P(x_{p},y_{k})-1+P(x_{j})-P(x_{j},y_{i}))

P^{UB}(y_{i_{x_{j}}},y_{k},x_{p})=\min(P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})-P(x_{j},y_{i}),P(x_{p},y_{k}))

Proof.

We show that
(
Li and Pearl, 2024
)
’s upper and lower bounds for the probability of causation are equivalent to our bounds in Theorems
D.3
and
D.4
, as follows.

(40)

\displaystyle P^{UB}(y_{i_{x_{j}}},y_{k},x_{p})

\displaystyle=\min(P(x_{p},y_{k}),P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})-P(x_{j},y_{i}))

\displaystyle P^{UB}(y_{i_{x_{j}}}\mid y_{k},x_{p})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\min(P(x_{p},y_{k}),P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})-P(x_{j},y_{i}))}{P(x_{p},y_{k})}

\displaystyle=\min(1,\dfrac{P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})-P(x_{j},y_{i})}{P(x_{p},y_{k})})

\displaystyle=\min(1,\dfrac{P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})-P(y_{i_{x_{j}}},x_{j})}{P({y_{k}}_{x_{p}},x_{p})})

\displaystyle=\min(1,\dfrac{P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})-P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})\cdot P(x_{j})}{P({y_{k}}_{x_{p}})\cdot P(x_{p})})

\displaystyle=\min(1,\dfrac{P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})}{P({y_{k}}_{x_{p}})})\text{ because $P(x_{p})=1$ and $P(x_{j})=0$}

which is equivalent to the upper bound in Theorem
D.3
where
x_{p}=(s_{t},a_{t})
,
y_{k}=s_{t+1}
,
x_{j}=(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
and
y_{i}=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}
.

(41)

\displaystyle P^{LB}(y_{i_{x_{j}}},y_{k},x_{p})

\displaystyle=\max(0,P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})+P(x_{p},y_{k})-1+P(x_{j})-P(x_{j},y_{i}))

\displaystyle P^{LB}(y_{i_{x_{j}}}\mid y_{k},x_{p})

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\max(0,P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})+P(x_{p},y_{k})-1+P(x_{j})-P(x_{j},y_{i}))}{P(x_{p},y_{k})}

\displaystyle=\max(0,\dfrac{P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})+P(x_{p},y_{k})-1+P(x_{j})-P(x_{j},y_{i})}{P(x_{p},y_{k})})

\displaystyle=\max(0,\dfrac{P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})+P(y_{k_{x_{p}}},x_{p})-1+P(x_{j})-P(y_{i_{x_{j}}},x_{j})}{P({y_{k}}_{x_{p}},x_{p})})

\displaystyle=\max(0,\dfrac{P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})+P(y_{k_{x_{p}}})\cdot P(x_{p})-1+P(x_{j})-P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})\cdot P(x_{j})}{P({y_{k}}_{x_{p}})\cdot P(x_{p})})

\displaystyle=\max(0,\dfrac{P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})+P(y_{k_{x_{p}}})-1}{P({y_{k}}_{x_{p}})})\text{ because $P(x_{p})=1$ and $P(x_{j})=0$}

\displaystyle=\max(0,\dfrac{P(y_{i_{x_{j}}})-(1-P(y_{k_{x_{p}}}))}{P({y_{k}}_{x_{p}})})

which is equivalent to the lower bound in Theorem
D.4
where
x_{p}=(s_{t},a_{t})
,
y_{k}=s_{t+1}
,
x_{j}=(\widetilde{s},\widetilde{a})
and
y_{i}=\widetilde{s}^{\prime}
.

∎

Appendix F
Training Details

Our algorithm was executed on a 128-core machine with an Intel Xeon CPU and 512 GB RAM. The interval CFMDP generation part of the algorithm is implemented in Python 3.10, and the value iteration algorithm over the interval CFMDP was implemented in Julia 1.9.4, using the Julia Interval MDP package (v.0.6.0)
(
Mathiesen et al., 2024
)
. Most of the algorithm was run single-threaded, except for generating the Sepsis and Aircraft interval CFMDPs and Gumbel-max SCM CFMDPs, which were run in parallel with
32
threads.

Appendix G
Environment Details

Below, we provide brief descriptions of each environment used in our experiments.

GridWorld

The GridWorld MDP is a
4\times 4
grid where an agent must navigate from the top-left corner to the goal state in the bottom-right corner, avoiding a dangerous terminal state in the centre. At each step, the agent can move up, down, left, or right, but there is a small probability (controlled by hyperparameter
p
) of moving in an unintended direction. As the agent nears the goal, the reward for each state increases, culminating in a reward of
+100
for reaching the goal. Entering the dangerous state results in a penalty of
-100
. We use two versions of GridWorld: a less stochastic version with
p=0.9
(i.e.,
90
% chance of moving in the chosen direction) and a more stochastic version with
p=0.4
.

Sepsis

The Sepsis MDP
(
Oberst and Sontag, 2019
)

simulates trajectories of Sepsis patients. Each state consists of four vital signs (heart rate, blood pressure, oxygen concentration, and glucose levels), categorised as low, normal, or high. At each time step, three treatments can be toggled on or off, yielding a total of 8 possible actions. Unlike
Oberst and Sontag (2019)
, we scale rewards based on the number of out-of-range vital signs, between
-1000
(patient dies) and
1000
(patient discharged).

Frozen Lake

The Frozen Lake MDP is a PRISM environment
(
Kwiatkowska et al., 2011
)
adapted from
(
Gross et al., 2022
)
. It consists of a
4\times 4
grid where an agent moves from the top-left corner to the goal in the bottom-right corner while avoiding several holes. As the PRISM environment is formulated as a reachability problem, we adapt it to a reward-based setting: the agent receives a reward proportional to its distance from the goal, a
+100
reward for reaching the goal, and a
-100
penalty for falling into a hole. Additionally, the environment has a terminal state (reachable from the goal and hole states) with a reward of
0
.

Aircraft

The Aircraft MDP is also a PRISM environment
(
Kwiatkowska et al., 2011
)
, adapted from
(
Suilen et al., 2022
)
. In this environment, two aircraft approach each other, with one controlled by the agent. Both aircraft can increase, decrease, or maintain their current altitude with a fixed probability of success. As the PRISM environment is formulated as a reachability problem, we adapt it to a reward-based setting: the agent receives a reward inversely proportional to how close it is to the other aircraft, a
+100
reward for reaching the goal, and a
-100
penalty for colliding with the other aircraft.

Appendix H
Additional Experiments

We observe similar results for the Frozen Lake experiments as with the GridWorld and Sepsis experiments. For the slightly suboptimal trajectory, the interval CFMDP follows the observed policy exactly, as it cannot guarantee higher rewards than those observed. In contrast, Gumbel-max policy deviates from the observed trajectory: while it can achieve higher rewards, it can also attain much lower rewards than the observation. For the almost catastrophic and catastrophic paths, both policies improve upon the observed trajectory. They are identical for the almost catastrophic path, but for the catastrophic path, the interval CFMDP policy demonstrates slightly better robustness, as indicated by the higher lower end of its error bars, compared to the Gumbel-max policy.

Figure 9
.
Average instant reward of CF paths induced by policies on Frozen Lake. Error bars denote the standard deviation in reward at each time step.

In the Aircraft experiment, we select the observed trajectories slightly differently from the other experiments, because in this environment the aircraft are guaranteed to either collide or the agent will reach the goal. Therefore, we define the slightly suboptimal path as a trajectory that reaches the goal but accumulates less reward than the optimal path, and the almost catastrophic path as a trajectory where the aircraft collide but accumulates higher reward than the catastrophic path.

Since the slightly suboptimal path still reaches the goal, neither the interval CFMDP nor the Gumbel-max policy deviates from the observed trajectory, as they cannot guarantee higher cumulative rewards by doing so. For the almost catastrophic and catastrophic paths, both policies improve upon the observed trajectories and achieve identical rewards. This is likely because all policies that reach the goal will achieve very similar rewards: under the environment constraints, once a policy reaches the goal without collision, its value function will closely match that of other effective policies, as they all require the same number of timesteps to reach the goal.

Table 8
.
Average worst-case counterfactual
V(s_{0})
for the ICFMDP policies (given varying assumptions) over
100
randomly sampled observed trajectories.

We also further examine the impact of the counterfactual stability and counterfactual monotonicity assumptions on the robustness of the approach by repeating the experiment from Section
6.3
under different assumptions. We take an additional
100
randomly sampled paths, and for each path, we derive ICFMDPs under three settings: (i) both counterfactual stability and counterfactual monotonicity, (ii) counterfactual stability only, and (iii) no assumptions. Table
8
reports the average worst-case counterfactual value
V(s_{0})
of the policies derived for each corresponding ICFMDP.

As expected, relaxing the assumptions leads to a decrease in the worst-case value
V(s_{0})
, due to the wider counterfactual transition bounds. However, in most environments, this performance drop is relatively small. This is consistent with Table
5
, which shows that the counterfactual transition bounds, on average, are only marginally tightened by the additional assumptions. In the Aircraft environment, the performance drop is greater, which we suspect is due to the specific transitions that have been affected by the addition of the counterfactual monotonicity assumption, as empirically we observe that many transitions leading to terminal states become significantly tighter when counterfactual monotonicity is imposed.

Nevertheless, across all environments, the worst-case performance of the derived policies over their respective ICFMDPs remains higher than the worst-case performance of the Gumbel-max SCM reported in Table
4
, demonstrating the robustness of our approach with and without assumptions.
